## Page 1


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S
ITERATED INTEGRALS AND HOMOTOPY TRANSFER
KAI CIELIEBAK AND EVGENY VOLKOV
Abstract. We develop the analytical foundations for integrals over configura-
tion spaces used to relate chain-level S1-equivariant string topology to pertur-
bative Chern-Simons theory. As an application, we prove that the composition
of Chen’s iterated integral with homotopy transfer intertwines the involutive
Lie bialgebra structures on homology.

1. Introduction
Let M be a closed, connected, oriented n-dimensional manifold and Λ = C∞(S1, M)
its free loop space. In their seminal paper [4] and its sequel [38], M. Chas and
D. Sullivan introduced operations on the homology of Λ which go under the name
string topology. In the equivariant setting, they consist of the string bracket µS1 and

For applications in symplectic topology (see e.g. [17, 13]), it became important
to establish the underlying structure on the chain level. This structure was alge-
braically described in [10] under the name IBL∞-structure. Moreover, an approach
was outlined for realizing this structure in the simply connected case. It is based
on Chen’s iterated integrals [5, 6], which give rise to a homomorphism

Remark 1.3. A result very similar to Theorem 1.1 has been obtained previously and
independently by Naef and Willwacher in [36]. Their approach builds for simply
connected M on a finite dimensional Poincar´e duality model for Ω∗(M) provided by
Lambrechts and Stanley [30], and for non-simply connected M on a dgca model for
the configuration space of points on M constructed by Campos and Willwacher [2].
By contrast, our approach is more analytic, working directly with configuration
space integrals and variants of Chen’s iterated integrals in the smooth setting.
Remark 1.4. Fukaya [17] has suggested a different approach to chain-level string
topology via de Rham chains. This approach has the advantage of not requiring
simple connectivity; it has been successfully implemented by Irie [23] in the non-
equivariant case.
Remark 1.5. The string bracket and cobracket actually lift to HS1
∗(Λ)/χ(M)HS1
∗(q0),
where χ(M) is the Euler characteristic of M (see §4), while the the operations
pm
2,1,0 = p2,1,0 and pm
1,2,0 exist on non-reduced homology H(Bcyc∗H, pm
1,1,0). How-
ever, according to [14, Theorem 3.5], the Chen map ¯Jλ∗in (1) only becomes an
isomorphism for simply connected M if we take homology relative to q0 on the left
hand side and reduced cohomology on the right hand side.
Structure of the paper.
The proof of Theorem 1.1 requires a combination of
algebraic, topological, and analytic techniques. Accordingly, this paper consists of
four parts, of which the first three are largely independent.
I. Algebra (§§2–3).
Here we recall the necessary definitions and facts about cochain
complexes and DGAs with pairings, dIBL-algebras, and A∞-algebras.
II. Topology (§§4–5).
This part concerns string topology and Chen’s iterated in-
tegrals. In §4.2 we determine the possible domains of definition for non-equivariant
and equivariant string topology operations. In §4.4 we present a chain level defini-
tion of the loop coproduct which is suitable for combining it with Chen’s iterated
integrals and homotopy transfer.
III. Analysis (§§6–10).
This part is the heart of the paper. Its results may be
of independent interest; for example, they also serve as the analytic underpinnings
for [15].
We begin in §6 with a formula relating pullback and fibre integration
of integrable differential forms.
This is followed by a discussion of propagators
in §7. In §8 we recall oriented real blow-ups and proper transforms and develop an
abstract setting for Stokes’ theorem. In §9 we apply this to generalized configura-
tion spaces associated to graphs. Building on results of Paw lucki on semi-analytic
sets [37], we prove Stokes’ theorem for such spaces. We also establish a general
vanishing result for integrals over hidden faces, generalizing earlier such results due
to Kontsevich [28] and others (see e.g. [1, 3]). In §10 we apply the results of §9 to
the configuration spaces that are relevant for the proof of the main theorem.


## Page 4


4
KAI CIELIEBAK AND EVGENY VOLKOV
IV. Proof (§§11–14).
Here we combine the results of the first three parts to prove
Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2. We begin in §11 with a detailed discussion of ribbon
graphs, labellings and their extensions, and operations on graphs. In §12 we define
integrals over configuration spaces associated to graphs and establish their main
properties. In §13 we use configuration space integrals to define the chain map and
chain homotopies entering the proof and derive their main properties. The actual
proof is contained in §14.
Acknowledgements.
This article is part of the second author’s habilitation
thesis. We thank T. Ekholm, M. Hutchings and J. Latschev for valuable feedback.
2. Cochain complexes and differential graded algebras with pairings
In this section we collect some basic notions and facts about cochain complexes and
differential graded algebras with pairings. See [10, 11] for more background.

2.2. Cochain complexes with pairing. A cochain complex (A, d) is a graded
vector space A with a differential d of degree 1. A pairing of degree n ∈Z on (A, d)
is a bilinear form (·, ·): A × A →R which for all homogeneous x, y ∈A satisfies the
degree condition
(x, y) ̸= 0
=⇒
deg x + deg y = n,
graded symmetry
(x, y) = (−1)deg x deg y(y, x),
(10)
and compatibility with the differential
(11)
(dx, y) = (−1)1+deg x(x, dy).
We write x ⊥y if (x, y) = 0 and say that x, y are orthogonal. The subcomplex of
elements of A orthogonal to a given subcomplex B ⊂A will be denoted by
B⊥:= {x ∈A | x ⊥B}.
We call a pairing (·, ·): A × A →R nondegenerate if the induced map
A −→Hom(A, R),
x 7−→(x, ·)
is injective, and perfect if it is an isomorphism.
Observe that a nonnegatively
graded cochain complex with a perfect pairing is finite dimensional. Following the
terminology in [10], a cochain complex with a perfect pairing is called cyclic. If a
perfect pairing restricts to a subcomplex as a perfect pairing, then the subcomplex
is called cyclic.
Propagators and symmetric projections.
Next we recall some notions and
facts from [11]. Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cochain complex with pairing. A projection on
A is a degree 0 chain map π: A →A which satisfies π◦π = π. We say that a degree
−1 map P : A →A is a homotopy operator if the map −(d ◦P + P ◦d): A →A is
a projection. Every homotopy operator P determines a projection
πP := 1l + d ◦P + P ◦d: A −→A
which is a quasi-isomorphism, i.e., the induced map on cohomology H(πP ): H(A) →
H(A) is an isomorphism. Given a projection π: A →A which is a quasi-isomorphism,
we say that a degree −1 map P : A →A is a homotopy operator with respect to π
if it is a homotopy operator and πP = π, so that
(12)
d ◦P + P ◦d = π −1l.
We say that a homotopy operator P : A →A is a propagator if it satisfies the
symmetry property
(13)
(Px, y) = (−1)deg x(x, Py).
The associated projection πP : A →A is then symmetric:
(πP x, y) = (x, πP y).
Given a subcomplex B ⊂A, we say that a projection π: A →A is onto B if
im π = B, and we identify π with the induced surjection π: A ↠B in this case. A
homotopy operator P : A →A with respect to a projection π: A ↠B exists if and
only if π is a quasi-isomorphism. In the case with pairing we have

Lemma 2.2 ([11, Lemma 2.2]). Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cyclic cochain complex and
B ⊂A a subcomplex. A propagator P with respect to a projection π: A ↠B exists
if and only if π is symmetric and a quasi-isomorphism.
If (A, d, (·, ·)) is a cyclic cochain complex, then any quasi-isomorphic cyclic sub-
complex B ⊂A admits a unique symmetric projection πB : A ↠B by sending the
orthogonal complement B⊥to 0. Lemma 2.2 now implies
Corollary 2.3. Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cyclic cochain complex and B ⊂A a quasi-
isomorphic cyclic subcomplex. Then there exists a propagator P : A →A such that
im πP = B.
□
Harmonic subspaces.
Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cochain complex with pairing. In
view of (11), the pairing descends to its homology H(A). In this subsection we
consider the case that H(A) is finite dimensional and the induced pairing on H(A)
is nondegenerate, so H(A) becomes a cyclic cochain complex with trivial differential.
Our main example for this will be the de Rham complex (Ω∗(M), d, (·, ·)) of a closed
oriented manifold with the intersection pairing (33).
By a harmonic subspace H we mean any complement of im d in ker d, so that
ker d = H ⊕im d.
Lemma 2.4. Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cochain complex with pairing such that H(A) is
finite dimensional and the induced pairing on H(A) is nondegenerate. Let H ⊂ker d
be a harmonic subspace. Then we get a direct sum decomposition
(14)
A = H ⊕H⊥
with
H⊥∩ker d = im d.
The projection πH : A →A onto H along H⊥is symmetric, and it is a quasi-
isomorphism as a map A →H.
Proof. Nondegeneracy of the pairing on H(A) and the fact that every cohomology
class has a unique harmonic representative implies that the restriction of the pairing
to H is nondegenerate. Pick a basis {ea} of H and define its dual basis {eb} of H
by (ea, eb) = δb
a. Then
πH : A →A,
πH(x) :=
X
a
(ea, x)ea
is a projection with image H and kernel H⊥, which shows the first equation in (14).
The inclusion H⊥∩ker d ⊃im d is obvious. For the converse inclusion, consider
x ∈H⊥∩ker d. In view of (11) this implies x ⊥H ⊕im d = ker d, and therefore
0 = (x, y) = ([x], [y]) for all y ∈ker d, where [x], [y] denote the cohomology classes.
By nondegeneracy of the pairing on homology this implies [x] = 0, hence x ∈im d.
This proves the second equation in (14), and the asserted properties of πH follow
directly from this.
□
We combine Corollary 2.3 and Lemma 2.4 to get the following statement.
Corollary 2.5. Let (A, d, (·, ·)) be a cochain complex with pairing such that H(A) is
finite dimensional and the induced pairing on H(A) is nondegenerate. Let H ⊂ker d
be a harmonic subspace. Then there exists a propagator P : A →A such that its
projection πP is exactly the orthogonal projection onto H.
□

2.3. IBL∞and dIBL structures. Here we recall from [10] the notions of IBL∞
structures and their Maurer-Cartan elements, and spell them out in the special case
of dIBL structures relevant to this paper.
IBL∞-structures.
Consider a Z-graded R-vector space C. For k ≥1 we define
the k-fold symmetric product
EkC := C[1]⊗k/Sk
as the quotient by the algebraic action of the symmetric group Sk.
According
to [10], an IBL∞structure of degree d ∈Z on C is a collection of linear maps
pk,ℓ,g : EkC →EℓC,
k ≥1, ℓ≥1, g ≥0
of degree
|pk,ℓ,g| = −2d(k + g −1) −1
satisfying a series of quadratic relations which can formally be written as ˆp ◦ˆp = 0
for the generating series ˆp of the operations pk,ℓ,g. We will spell out these relations
below in the special case of a dIBL structure relevant for this paper.
In [10], the notions of IBL∞-morphisms and their homotopies are defined, and it is
proved that quasi-isomorphisms are homotopy equivalences. Morphisms from the
ground field give rise to the notion of a Maurer-Cartan element. This is a collection
of elements
mℓ,g ∈bEℓC,
ℓ≥1, g ≥0
in a suitable completion bEℓC of EℓC of degree
|mℓ,g| = −2d(g −1)
satisfying a series of quadratic relations which can formally be written as ˆp(em) =
0. A Maurer-Cartan element m = {mℓ,g} gives rise to a twisted IBL∞-structure
pm = {pm
k,ℓ,g}. Again, we will spell this out in the relevant special case to which we
now turn.
dIBL structures.
Let
τalg : C[1]⊗2 →C[1]⊗2,
Nalg : C[1]⊗3 →C[1]⊗3
be the algebraic action of the flip of two elements and the symmetrization operator
defined in §2.1. A dIBL-structure of degree d is an IBL∞-structure consisting of
only the three operations
p1,1,0 : C[1] →C[1],
p2,1,0 : C[1]⊗2 →C[1],
p1,2,0 : C[1] →C[1]⊗2
(with all operations zero) satisfying the symmetry properties
p2,1,0 ◦τalg = p2,1,0,
τalg ◦p1,2,0 = p1,2,0.
Their degrees are
|p1,1,0| = −1,
|p2,1,0| = −2d −1,
|p1,2,0| = −1

p1,2,0 ◦p2,1,0 + (p2,10 ⊗id) ◦(id ⊗p1,2,0) + (id ⊗p2,10) ◦(p1,2,0 ⊗id) = 0,
p2,1,0 ◦p1,2,0 = 0.
The first relation says that p1,1,0 is a differential, the next two relations show that
p2,1,0 and p1,2,0 descend to homology H(C[1], p1,1,0), and the last four relations
mean that they define on homology the structure of an involutive Lie bialgebra,
see [10].

2.4. Differential graded algebras with pairings. In this subsection we recall
the Hochschild and cyclic complexes of a differential graded algebra (DGA), and
the canonical Maurer-Cartan element of a cyclic DGA.
DGAs and their Hochschild and cyclic complexes.
A differential graded
algebra (DGA) (A, d, ∧) is a nonnegatively graded cochain complex (A, d) equipped
with an associative product ∧: A × A →A of degree 0 satisfying the Leibniz rule
d(x ∧y) = dx ∧y + (−1)deg xx ∧dy.
Let us recall the definition of its Hochschild and cyclic complexes, see [33] and [14,

3. A∞-algebras
In this section we recall some background on A∞-algebras, see e.g. [27], and gener-
alize some notions of Section §2.1 to this setting.
Notation. Throughout this paper we abuse notation by writing the sign (−1)deg a
associated to an element as (−1)a, the sign of a permutation σ as (−1)σ, etc. This
becomes a bit awkward when we combine sign exponents to expressions like a + η,
but it should always be clear what is meant.
Let A = L
i≥0 Ai be a nonnegatively graded R-vector space. A sequence m =

holds:

Here we adopt the convention to write operations to the left of elements and stipu-
late that moving an element x past an operation of degree q produces a sign (−1)q|x|.
The pair (A, m) is then called an A∞-algebra. Note that m1 is a differential on A.
Let (A, m) and (B, n) be two A∞-algebras. A collection f = {fk}k≥1 of linear maps
fk : A[1]⊗k −→B[1]
of degree 0 is called an A∞-morphism if the following identity holds for all k ≥1:
X

The map f1 : A[1] →B[1] is called the linear part of f. Note that f1 is a chain map
with respect to the differentials m1 on A[1] and n1 on B[1]. A morphism f is called
a quasi-isomorphism if its linear part induces an isomorphism on homology, and in
this case the two A∞- algebras are called quasi-isomorphic. It follows from [34, Sec-
tion 10.4.3] that being quasi-isomorphic is an equivalence relation. For the notions
of homotopy of A∞-morphisms and homotopy equivalence see [27, Section 3.7]. The
following classical result can be found in [32]. See also [40, Theorem 6].
Theorem 3.1. Two A∞-algebras are quasi-isomorphic if and only if they are ho-
motopy equivalent.

3.5. Cyclic A∞-algebras and Maurer-Cartan elements. A cyclic A∞-algebra
is an A∞-algebra (A, m) endowed with a pairing (·, ·) of degree n such that (A, m1, (·, ·))
is a cyclic cochain complex in the sense of §2.2 and for all x0, x1, . . . xk, k ≥2, we
have
(43)
⟨mk(x1, . . . , xk), x0⟩= (−1)|x0|(|x1|+···+|xk|)⟨mk(x0, . . . , xk−1), xk⟩.
Here |xi| are the shifted degrees in A[1] and ⟨·, ·⟩is the cyclic pairing defined
in (21). Note that by cyclicity of (A, m1, (·, ·)) this relation also holds for k = 1.
The definition of a cyclic A∞-algebra is consistent with that of a cyclic DGA, i.e.,
a DGA A is cyclic if and only if the corresponding A∞-algebra A is cyclic.

Proof. We will sketch the proof and refer to [41] for details, in particular regarding
signs. Consider a labelled trivalent planar tree Γ with s leaves, where a labelling

4. Equivariant and non-equivariant string topology
In this section we recall the algebraic structures on equivariant and non-equivariant
loop space homology and discuss their relation. All homology groups will be with

Remark 4.4. For non-simply connected M, the extension λ of the coproduct λ is
not unique but depends on the choice of a generic vector field on M. For example,
for M = S1 there are two choices corresponding to the two classes of nowhere
vanishing vector fields [12], and this ambiguity persists for the cobracket λS1. Since
the ambiguity lies in the constant loops, which are anihilated by Chen’s iterated
integrals, everything in the sequel (in particular the discussion in 14) will be true
for any choice of λ and λS1.

Definition 1. A smooth map f : B →Λ is called nondegenerate if the following
conditions are satisfied (where transversality is meant stratawise on B):
(i) the restriction ef|B×(0,1) is transverse to the diagonal ∆⊂M × M;
(ii) the map vf is transverse to the zero section M ⊂TM.
Lemma 4.5. The set of nondegenerate maps f : B →Λ is open in C∞(B ×S1, M)
with respect to the C2-topology, and the set of real analytic nondegenerate maps is
dense with respect to the C∞-topology.
Proof. Openness of condition (ii) in the C2-topology is clear; openness of nondegen-
eracy follows because (ii) implies (i) along B × {0, 1} (see the proof of Lemma 4.6
below) and (ii) is open away from a neighbourhood of B × {0, 1}.
The Thom
transversality theorem (see [9, Theorem 2.3.2]) yields density of smooth nondegen-
erate maps in the C∞-topology, and by [8, Theorem 5.53] smooth nondegenerate
maps can be C∞-approximated by real analytic ones.

5. Chen’s iterated integrals
Let M be a closed connected oriented manifold. In this section we define various
versions of Chen’s iterated integrals, relating chain complexes built out of the de
Rham complex Ω∗(M) to the singular chain complex of the free loop space Λ =
C∞(S1, M).
The discussion follows [14].
We borrow the notation about loops
spaces from §4, and about Hochschild and cyclic complexes from §2.4. Throughout
this section B, Bj denote compact connected oriented manifolds with corners.

6. Fibre integration
In this section B, Bi, E, Ei, F denote compact oriented manifolds, possibly with
corners. All integrals are understood in the Lebesgue sense.
Definition 2. A measurable differential form on B is a measurable section ω of the
bundle of exterior forms Λ∗TB →B. We call ω integrable if for every smooth test
form γ ∈Ω∗(B) the integral
R
B ω ∧γ exists. The space of integrable forms on
B will be denoted by Ω∗
int(B). Any integrable form can be uniquely represented
as a sum over p = 0, . . . , dim B of integrable forms of degree p.
Similarly, any
integrable form on B1 ×B2 can be represented as a sum over p = 0, . . . , dim B1 and
q = 0, . . . , dim B2 of integrable forms of bidegree (p, q).


## Page 32


32
KAI CIELIEBAK AND EVGENY VOLKOV
Remark 6.1. (a) Since B is compact, integrability of ω is equivalent to the following:
in every coordinate patch, the coefficients of ω are integrable functions.
(b) If ω has top degree, then we can write it as ϕ volB for a smooth volume form
volB and a measurable function ϕ, and ω is integrable if and only if ϕ is integrable.
(c) In the subsequent discussion, we will always speak of explicit forms and not their
equivalence classes modulo subsets of measure zero. The reason is that modifying
a form on a subset of measure zero can change the integral of its pullback under a
smooth map.
Let us fix a (compact oriented) manifold F of dimension d = dim F and a positive
smooth volume form volF on F.
We also also fix a positive integer n.
By an
F-bundle we will mean a smooth fibre bundle p : E →B with fibre F over a
(compact oriented) n-dimensional base manifold B. The total space E inherits an
orientation according to the convention “fibre first, base second”. Our goal is to
define the pushforward p∗ω of integrable forms ω on E.
We will reduce this to the following special case. By a ball we will mean a manifold
diffeomorphic to the closed unit ball in Rn. For 0 ≤k ≤n, a basis of k-forms on
B is a collection of αI ∈Ωk(B) for multi-indices I of length |I| = k which gives a
basis of ΛkTqB at each q ∈B. For the trivial F-bundle p : F × B →B over a ball
B, we can then write each integrable (k + d)-form ω on F × B uniquely as
(80)
ω =

with the following property: for each point (ξ, q) ∈F × B and a basis X1, . . . , Xd
of TξF we have
ωrest(X1, . . . , Xdim F , ·) = 0.

8. Blow-ups and Stokes’ theorem
In this section we recall background on manifolds with corners and blow-ups and
develop an abstract setting for Stokes’ theorem.
8.1. Manifolds with corners. Throughout this section, let X be a manifold with
corners. Recall that this is is defined like a manifold, with open subsets of Rn
replaced by open subsets of [0, ∞)n; see e.g. [25]. For k ≥0 we denote by ∂kX its
codimension k stratum (where exactly k of the local coordinates are zero), and by
∂≥kX the (closed) union of strata of codimension at least k. The interior ∂0X will
also sometimes be denoted by X0.
Nice submanifolds.
By a “closed submanifold” we will mean a submanifold
which is closed as a subset (such as R ⊂C). We say that a closed submanifold
C (possibly with boundary and corners) of X is nice if C ∩∂kX = ∂kC and C is
transverse to ∂kX for all k. In particular, a nice submanifold of a manifold without
boundary has no boundary. The natural inclusion of a nice submanifold will be
called a nice embedding.
Transverse collections.
Next, we will define transversality for a finite collection
of nice submanifolds Ca of X. Assume first that X and the Ca have no boundary.
Then C1 and C2 intersect transversely if TpC1 + TpC2 = TpX for all p ∈C1 ∩C2.
Assume now inductively that transversality has been defined for any collection of
m −1 submanifolds, for some m ≥3. Then we call a collection of m subman-
ifolds transverse if any subcollection of m −1 submanifolds is transverse and its
intersection is transverse to the remaining submanifold.


## Page 41

41
Let now X be a manifold with corners. By a boundary component of X we will mean
the closure of a codimension 1 stratum (which is again a manifold with corners).
Then
∂X := ∂≥1X =
[

where ∂bX, b ∈B, are the boundary components. The following remark will be
used repeatedly in the sequel.
Remark 8.1. By definition of a manifold with corners, near each p ∈∂kX, k ≥1,
we can slightly extend X and the boundary components ∂bX that meet at p to
manifolds without boundary such that the extended ∂bX intersect in the extended
X at p like k coordinate hyperplanes intersect at 0 in Rd. It is easy to see that a
closed smooth submanifold C ⊂X is nice if and only if near each point p ∈∂kX
it admits an extension such that the extended C together with the k extended
boundary components forms a transverse family near p.
Since the notion of transversality is a local one, it is enough to define it near each
p ∈X. If p ∈∂kX for some k ≥1, then we say that a finite collection {Ca}a∈A
of nice submanifolds of X intersects transversely at p if the combined collection of
the extended Ca’s and the extended ∂bX’s in the extended X from Remark 8.1
intersects transversely near p in the above sense without boundary.
In the following, by a manifold with corners with a transverse collection
(X, C) = (X, {Ca}a∈A)
we will mean a manifold with corners X with a transverse collection of nice sub-
manifolds Ca ⊂X indexed by a finite set A.
Let (X, C) be manifold with corners with a transverse collection, and
g : Y −→X
be a smooth map from a manifold with corners. We say that the map g is transverse
to the collection C = {Ca} if its graph
gr(g) = {(y, g(y))} ⊂Y × X
together with the Y × Ca forms a transverse collection.
Remark 8.2. For technical reasons it will be convenient to formally add to a trans-
verse collection {Ca}a∈A the collection of boundary components, i.e., to consider
the extended collection {Ca}a∈A ⨿{∂bX}b∈B. We reindex the extended collection
using the set
A∂:= A ⨿B
as {Ca}a∈A∂by setting Ca := ∂aX for a ∈B. This way, many statements about nice
submanifolds of a manifold with corners will formally follow from the corresponding
statements for manifolds without boundary.
Remark 8.3. For a manifold with corners with a transverse collection (X, C), near
every point p of X there is a chart straightening all Ca’s and boundary components
passing through p.

Remark 8.5. Blowing up a manifold with corners along its boundary strata does
not change the manifold. The reason to do this is the following. Once we include
boundary strata in the blow-up locus, we can extend the well-known theorem that
blowing up a manifold without boundary along a transverse collection is a manifold
with corners to the case of the ambient manifold having boundary and corners.
Remark 8.6. For p ∈P +NJ the kernel ker dpπ of the blow-down map equals the
tangent space to the fibre of the product sphere bundle πJ : P +NJ −→XJ.

PT(Z) := Closure(π−1(Z \ C)) ⊂Bl+(X, C).

a sequence (xn) ⊂X\C with π−1(xn) →q. We call such a sequence approximating.

8.4. A general setup for Stokes’ theorem. In this subsection we introduce a
general setup for Stokes’ theorem.
Manifolds with quasi-regular boundary.
We begin with some definitions
from Pawlucki’s article [37].
Definition 4. Let L be a topological space and ∂q−regL ⊂L a closed subset. We
say that (L, ∂q−regL) (or simply L) is a manifold with quasi-regular boundary if the
following holds: the difference L \ ∂q−regL is an oriented manifold; for each point
p ∈∂q−regL there exists an open neighbourhood U ⊂L of p such that U \ ∂q−regL
consists of m ≥1 connected components {U j
0}m
j=1 and each U j := U j
0⨿(∂q−regL∩U)
is a C1-manifold with boundary, with interior Uj
0 and boundary (∂q−regL∩U). The
U j are called the local regular components at p. The multiplicity m can depend on p
but must be locally constant. The open subset of ∂q−regL defined by the equation
m = 1 is denoted by ∂regL and called the regular boundary. If m = 1 constantly,
then we get the well-known notion of an oriented manifold with boundary.
Remark 8.9. (a) The orientability hypothesis in Definition 4 is included because it is
needed for our applications to Stokes’ theorem. Let us emphasize that in a manifold
with quasi-regular boundary the local regular components are only required to have
regularity C1 (whereas otherwise we usually assume all objects to be of class C∞).
(b) An example of a manifold with quasi-regular boundary is a graph, where the
multiplicity at a vertex is its degree. In the examples relevant in this paper, arising
from compactified configuration spaces, the multiplicity will actually be one. We
include it in the definition so that we can directly appeal to the results in [37].
An odd k-form on a manifold L is a k-form α on its orientation double cover eL with
τ ∗α = −α for the canonical involution τ : eL →eL. If k = dim L and α has compact
support, then it has a well-defined integral
R
L α. In this terminology, each local
regular component U j at p ∈∂q−regL induces an odd 0-form εj on ∂q−regL near p
whose value is 1 on the boundary orientation. The sums ε1 + · · · + εm at all p fit
together to a Z-valued odd 0-form ε on ∂q−regL.
Definition 5. Let (L, ∂q−regL) be a manifold with quasi-regular boundary and
(N, ∂N) be a manifold with corners.
We say that a map ι : (L, ∂q−regL) →
(N, ∂N) is an embedding (of a manifold with quasi-regular boundary into a mani-
fold with corners) if ι is injective, it restricts to an embedding between the interiors
L\∂q−regL →N \∂N, and it restricts to an embedding of a manifold with boundary
into a manifold with corners on each local regular component of L.

of Definition 5.

This lemma will be used as follows.
By definition, the quasi-regular boundary
∂q−regX is the set of all points with a certain property. While it is often difficult to
identify exactly all quasi-regular points of ∂X, sometimes there is an open subset
X1 of ∂q−regX for which we can prove equation (102). Then equation (103) allows
us to identify ∂q−regX up to a subset of {ε = 0} and a set of measure zero. For an
implementation of this idea see the proofs of Lemma 9.11 and Lemma 9.12.
Remark 8.15. (a) Stokes’ theorem is local. More precisely, if Stokes’ theorem holds
for the pair (Y, X), then for any open subset U of Y it also holds for (Y ∩U, X ∩U).
Conversely, given a pair (Y, X) and an open cover {Ui}i∈I of Y the following is
true: if Stokes’ theorem holds for all (Y ∩Ui, X ∩Ui), then it holds for (Y, X). The
proof is straightforward using a partition of unity.
(b) Suppose that Z is a manifold with corners, Y ⊂Z a nice submanifold, and
(Y, X) a pair. Then (Z, X) is also a pair, and Stokes’ theorem holds for (Y, X) if
and only if it holds for (Z, X).

9. Stokes’ theorem for configuration spaces
In this section we prove a version of Stokes’ theorem for configuration spaces asso-
ciated to graphs, building on results of Paw lucki [37] in the semi-analytic setting.
Moreover, we derive a vanishing result for integrals over hidden faces.
9.1. Graphs and their configurations spaces. We begin by briefly describing
a suitable class of graphs. See §11 for a more extensive discussion of graphs.
Let Γ be a finite (not necessarily connected) graph. Informally, this is a finite set of
vertices connected by edges. We also allow free edges adjacent to only one vertex
which we call leaves. A flag is then a pair of a vertex with an adjacent edge or leaf.
More formally, we define Γ as a finite set Flag of flags together with a collection Edge
of disjoint two-element subsets called edges and a decomposition of into disjoint
subsets called vertices. Flags not belonging to any edge are called leaves.
We assume that some (possibly empty) subset of the vertices are designated as
special, and we denote by Vert the set of nonspecial vertices. Flags adjacent to
special vertices will also be called special. We denote by dj (j ∈Vert) the valencies
of the nonspecial vertices, by d the total number of special flags, by e the number
of edges, and by s the number of leaves.
We order the nonspecial and special vertices, as well as the flags around each vertex.
This gives us the so-called vertex order on the set of flags. On the other hand, we
also order and orient the edges and order the leaves. This gives us the so-called
edge order on the flags. In the remainder of this section, by a graph we will mean a
graph with special vertices and chosen orderings as above (these choices correspond
to extended labellings in the terminology of §11.3).

9.3. Stokes’ theorem in the semi-analytic setting. Now we describe the main
result of Paw lucki [37] and apply it to our situation.
Analytic and semi-analytic sets.
Let us first describe a special case of the
setting of [37]. Let E be a finite dimensional real vector space. A subset A of E
is called semi-analytic if for each point of E there exists an open neighbourhood
U ⊂E and two collections of real analytic functions {fj
i , gj
i }j=1,...,q
i=1,...,p on U such that
A ∩U =
p[
i=1
q\
j=1
{f j
i = 0, gj
i > 0}.
A semi-analytic subset A of E is called analytic if the functions gj
i can be taken
constant equal to 1.
We call manifold with corners X analytic if the transition maps between manifold-
with-corner charts are restrictions of real analytic maps. We can then extend the
boundary components at a point p ∈∂kX by the corresponding coordinate hyper-
planes in a real analytic chart. We call a subset A ⊂X analytic if in real analytic


## Page 53


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S ITERATED INTEGRALS
53
charts it corresponds to the intersection of a quadrant with a real analytic subset
defined in a neighbourhood in Rn. This ensures that the intersection A ∩X0 is a
semi-analytic set. We call an analytic subset C ⊂X a nice analytic submanifold
if C ∩X0 is a submanifold, and near each point p ∈∂kX it admits an analytic
extension such that the extended C together with the k extended boundary com-
ponents forms a transverse family near p. A map between manifolds with corners
is called analytic if it locally extends to an analytic map between open subsets of
linear spaces.
Stokes’ theorem.
The following theorem corresponds to the main result in
Paw lucki [37].
Theorem 9.7. Let A0 ⊂E be a semi-analytic subset which is also an oriented d-
dimensional submanifold. Then Stokes’ theorem holds for the pair (E, Closure(A0)).
Proof. Note first that (E, A) is a pair as in Definition 6, so it remains to verify the
conditions in Definition 9. Integrability over A0 of every d-form α ∈Ωd(E) with
supp α∩A compact follows from Remark 8.13 and [37, Lemma 3.5] . Equation (102)
is the content of [37, Theorem 3.7].
□
We will apply this result to the following setting. Let X be a subset of a Euclidean
space F cut out by finitely many linear inhomogeneous inequalities,
X = {hi ≥0 for i = 1, . . . , K} ⊂F ∼= Rdim X.
We assume that the corresponding affine subspaces {hi = 0}i=1,...,K form a trans-
verse family, so that X is a manifold with corners and X0 = {hi > 0 for all i}.
Let
C = {Ca}a∈A
be a transverse family of (finitely many) nice submanifolds of X, each obtained by
intersecting a linear subspace with X.
Lemma 9.8. The manifold with corners Y := Bl+(X, C) carries a canonical ana-
lytic structure which agrees with the one induced from F on its interior Y0 ⊂F.
Proof. By Lemma 8.4, Y is a manifold with corners. We give it an analytic struc-
ture by equipping the sphere Sd−1 ⊂Rd with its standard analytic structure and
declaring the homeomorphism Φ : [0, ∞) × Sd−1 →eRd in the proof of Lemma 8.4
to be analytic. Since the second component of Φ sending (r, v) to rv ∈Rd is ana-
lytic, this analytic structure agrees with the one induced from F on Y0 ⊂F. Note
that local analytic manifold-with-corner charts are obtained from local analytic
coordinates (e.g. generalized polar coordinates) on the involved spheres.
□
Continuing the above setup, let Z ⊂X be an analytic subset such that Z0 =
Z ∩X0 ⊂X0 is an oriented submanifold.
Proposition 9.9. Stokes’ theorem holds for the pair (Bl+(X, C), PT(Z)).
Proof. As in Remark 8.2, we formally add the boundary faces {hi = 0}i=1,...,K
to the collection C to treat them on an equal footing.
Since by Remark 8.15
Stokes’ theorem is local, it suffices to prove it for a neighbourhood of every point of
Y := Bl+(X, C). By Lemma 9.8, for a sufficiently small such neighbourhood there
exists a homeomorphism Φ : U →V onto an open subset V ⊂[0, ∞)k×Rn−k whose

restriction Φ|U0 : U0 := U ∩Y0 ∼= X0 \ C →V0 := V ∩(0, ∞)k × Rn−k is analytic.
Thus A0 := Φ(Z0 \ C) ⊂V0 ⊂Rn is semi-analytic, so by Theorem 9.7 Stokes’
theorem holds for the pair (Rdim X, A) with A := Closure(A0) = Φ(U ∩PT(Z)),
and therefore for the pair (U, U ∩PT(Z)).
□

Chopping off trees.
Above we have discussed the attaching of trees. Now we
will discuss the converse operation. Let Γ be a graph as in §9.1.
Definition 11. A rooted subtree (T, r) is a connected subtree T ⊂Γ with a root
vertex r ∈T such that the following holds:
(i) T contains at least one edge.
(ii) T contains no special vertices of Γ except possibly r;
(iii) if T contains one flag of an edge l of Γ, then it also contains the other flag
of l;
(iv) if T contains one flag of a vertex v ̸= r of Γ, then it also contains all other
flags of v;
(v) (T, r) is maximal with properties (i)–(iii).
Remark 9.17. Thinking of a graph as a topological space, conditions (iii) and (iv)
say that T ⊂Γ is closed and T \ {r} ⊂Γ is open. Together with the maximality
condition (v), this implies that two distinct rooted subtrees can meet at most at
their root vertices. Hence, all rooted subtrees of Γ form a finite collection {(Ti, ri)}
meeting at most at their root vertices.
Let (T, r) be a rooted subtree of Γ. We define the graph Γ−obtained by chopping
off (T, r) from Γ as the graph obtained by removing from Γ all flags of T except
the root flag (which becomes a leaf of Γ−). Thus attaching the rooted tree T to Γ−
at this leaf gives back Γ. We denote by
(122)
Γcyc ⊂Γ
the unique subgraph obtained from Γ by first chopping off all rooted subtrees, and
then removing all nonspecial leaves from the resulting graph. See Figure 2. Note
that Γcyc contains all cycles of Γ, as well as all special vertices with their adjacent
flags.
Definition 12. An edge l of Γ is called doubly special if both of its flags are special.
Proposition 9.18. Let (YΓ, XΓ) be the basic pair associated by (107) to a graph

For a graph Γ, recall the definition of Γcyc from (122).
Definition 13. We say that a graph Γ admits cancellation if for every subgraph
Γ′ ⊂Γ the associated graph Γ′
cyc has either no edges or at least one 2-valent
nonspecial vertex as in Figure 1.
Remark 9.23. If Γ admits cancellation then it has no doubly special edges. (If l is
a doubly special edge, then the subgraph Γ′ consisting of l and its adjacent vertices
satisfies Γ′
cyc = Γ′ and violates Definition 13).
The following example of such a graph is central in [15].
Lemma 9.24. Let Γ be a connected trivalent ribbon graph without special vertices.
Then Γ admits cancellation if and only if it has at least one leaf.
Proof. Since Γ has no special vertices, we can omit the specification “nonspecial”
in the subsequent discussion.


## Page 63


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S ITERATED INTEGRALS
63
Assume first that Γ does not admit cancellation. Then there exists a subgraph
Γ′ ⊂Γ with at least one edge and without 2-valent vertices. Since each vertex
of Γ′
cyc is at most trivalent (since Γ′
cyc is a subgraph of Γ) and not univalent (by
definition of Γ′
cyc in terms of chopping off trees, using that it has at least one edge),
this implies that Γ′
cyc is trivalent. Since Γ is trivalent and connected, the inclusion
Γ′
cyc ⊂Γ cannot be strict and we get Γ′
cyc = Γ. By definition of Γ′
cyc, this implies
that Γ has no leaves.
Conversely, assume that Γ has no leaves. Then it has no rooted trees (because each
rooted tree must contain a leaf or a univalent vertex), and therefore Γcyc = Γ. Since
Γ is trivalent, it follows that Γcyc has at least one edge and no 2-valent vertices,
thus (with Γ′ = Γ) the graph Γ does not admit cancellation.
□
The following example will be crucial for the discussion of coproducts below. We
define a circular graph as a graph Γ whose ribbon surface ΣΓ is an annulus. It has
a unique embedded cycle such that Γ is obtained by attaching trees to this cycle.
Lemma 9.25. Let Γ be a circular graph with one special vertex. Then Γ admits
cancellation if and only if its cycle consists of more than one edge.
Proof. If the cycle of Γ consists of just one edge, then this edge is a self-loop
based at a certain vertex (special or nonspecial). Choose the subgraph Γ′ ⊂Γ in
Definition 13 to be the self-loop. Then Γ′ does not contain any rooted subtrees
in the sense of Definition 11. Thus, Γ′
cyc = Γ′ consists of just the above self-loop,
violating Definition 13.
Assume now that the cycle of Γ contains more than one edge. Then we have two
possibilities for the subgraph Γ′. The first possibility is that Γ′ does not contain
the cycle of Γ. Then the graph Γ′
cyc has no edges. The second possibility is that Γ′
contains the cycle of Γ. Then Γ′
cyc contains exactly the edges forming the cycle of Γ.
Since the cycle has at least two edges and at most one special vertex, it must contain
a 2-valent nonspecial vertex. Hence, Γ admits cancellation by Definition 13.
□
Now we are ready to formulate the main result of this section.
Proposition 9.26 (Vanishing for hidden faces). In the setting of Proposition 9.18,
assume in addition that Z ⊂M d × W is a nice submanifold and Γ admits cancel-
lation. Then for every η ∈Ωn−1(f
M 2) and compactly supported α ∈Ω∗(M s × W)
we have

Proof. By Remark 9.23, the graph Γ has no doubly special edges. Therefore, by
Corollary 9.22, each subset J ⊂Edge for which ∂q−reg

at least 2 elements. Proposition 9.18 (B) implies that ΓJ has no rooted subtrees,
so ΓJ is the union of ΓJcyc and all flags at special vertices of ΓJcyc. In particular,
ΓJcyc has at least two edges. Since Γ admits cancellation, it follows that ΓJcyc has
a 2-valent nonspecial vertex v. Then v is also a 2-valent nonspecial vertex of ΓJ,
and Corollary 9.5 yields vanishing of the integral.
□

main theorem.

least two edges. Thus Lemma 9.25 implies that Γ admits cancellation, and Propo-
sition 9.26 gives us vanishing of integrals over hidden faces. Stokes’ theorem holds
by Lemma 9.10.

11. Ribbon graphs
This section contains background material on ribbon graphs. It is structured as
follows. In §11.1 we introduce the basic notions concerning ribbon graphs, and
in §11.2 we define the six main types of graphs used in this paper. In §11.3 we
introduce (extended) labellings for ribbon graphs, the reordering map ¯RΓ, and the
sign exponent η3(Γ). In §11.4 we discuss automorphisms. In §11.5 we introduce
three operations on ribbon graphs: disjoint union, cutting, and gluing. In §11.6 we
introduce three more operations: duality, attaching a leg, and attaching a tree.
11.1. Basic definitions. In this paper, by a ribbon graph (or just a graph) Γ we
mean a finite graph with a cyclic ordering of the adjacent edges at each vertex
and with some degree 1 vertices removed.6 Note that if Γ is a tree then its ribbon
structure can equivalently be given by embedding it in the plane, so in this case
“planar” and “ribbon” are synonymous. We assume that at most one vertex is
removed from each edge, and we will refer to the edges with a vertex removed not
as edges but as leaves, so that an edge still ends in two vertices. We denote the
sets of vertices, edges and leaves of Γ by Vert(Γ), Edge(Γ) and Leaf(Γ), and their
cardinalities by
k = |Vert(Γ)|,
e = |Edge(Γ)|,
s = |Leaf(Γ)|.
We require k ≥1. A ribbon graph Γ can be thickened in a unique way (up to
orientation preserving homeomorphism) to a compact oriented surface ΣΓ with
boundary such that each leaf ends on the boundary ∂ΣΓ. We denote
ℓ= number of boundary components of ΣΓ,
g = genus of ΣΓ.
Note that the boundary components of ΣΓ induce additional structure on the set
of leaves: it gets subdivided into subsets according to the boundary components,
and each subset obtains a cyclic order according to the boundary orientation.
A flag in Γ is a pair (v, t) consisting of a vertex v and an adjacent edge or leaf t. A
flag (v, t) is called interior if t is an edge, and exterior if t is a leaf. We denote the
set of flags of Γ by Flag(Γ) and its cardinality by
f = |Flag(Γ)|.
6In contrast to [10] we do not require Γ to be connected.


## Page 75


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S ITERATED INTEGRALS
75
For our purposes it will be convenient to describe a ribbon graph in terms of its
flags: a leaf corresponds to a single flag x, an edge corresponds to an unordered pair
{x, y} of flags, and a vertex corresponds to a cyclically ordered tuple [x1, . . . , xd]
of flags. An oriented edge corresponds to an ordered pair (x, y), and a vertex with
an ordering of the adjacent edges corresponds to an ordered tuple (x1, . . . , xd). An
isomorphism Γ →Γ′ between ribbon graphs is a bijection Flag(Γ) →Flag(Γ′)
mapping leaves to leaves, vertices to vertices, edges to edges, and preserving the
cyclic orderings at the vertices. It induces a homeomorphism ΣΓ →ΣΓ′.
Marked ribbon graphs.
We say that a connected ribbon graph Γ is marked
if an edge is chosen, and o-marked if the marked edge is in addition oriented. A
(o-)marked graph will be denoted by (Γ, l) with l the (oriented) marked edge.
Special vertices.
A graph may have some chosen vertices called special ver-
tices. Flags adjacent to special vertices will be called special flags (they can be
either interior or exterior). An edge containing a special flag is called special, and
an edge containing two special flags is called doubly special. For certain types of
marked graphs (see below) with special vertices the marked edge can (and will) be
canonically oriented.
Rooted trees.
A root in a tree is a distinguished univalent vertex. By a rooted
tree we will mean a connected planar tree with a unique root.
11.2. The six types of graphs. Now we describe the six main types of graphs
used in this paper. They are all required to satisfy the following two conditions:
(145)
Each boundary component of ΣΓ has at least one leaf ending on it.
(146)
All nonspecial vertices of Γ are trivalent.
Special vertices are allowed to have any positive valency.
(s) Trivalent graphs.
Graphs of this type are connected trivalent ribbon graphs
without special vertices.
(o) Trivalent trees.
Graphs of this type are connected trivalent planar trees
without special vertices.
(i) Trees with one special vertex (see Figure 3).
Graphs Γ of this type are
connected planar trees with one special vertex S of degree d ≥1. Then Γ can be
represented as a union of d planar rooted trees with their roots glued together at
the special vertex S. We call them the rooted components of Γ. Figure 3 shows a
rooted component encircled in blue. Here and in the following special vertices are
drawn as red circles. Observe the inequality (with s the number of leaves)
(147)
d ≤s.
(ii) Trees with two special vertices (see Figure 4).
Graphs Γ of this type
are connected planar trees with two special vertices S1, S2 of degrees d1, d2 ≥1. We
consider the numbering of the special vertices S1, S2 part of the data of Γ. Then Γ
can be represented as a union of d1 −1 rooted trees with roots at S1, d2 −1 rooted
trees with roots at S2, and a connecting tree CΓ with two roots — one corresponding
to a special flag (called A) at S1 and the other to a special flag (called Z) at S2.
Observe the inequality
(148)
d1 + d2 ≤s + 2.

11.3. Labellings, edge and vertex orders.
Definition 14. Consider the following additional data on a ribbon graph Γ:
(i) a numbering of the boundary components of ΣΓ by 1, . . . , ℓ;
(ii) a numbering of the leaves ending on the b-th boundary component by
1, . . . , sb compatible with the cyclic order given by the orientation;
(iii) a numbering of the vertices;
(iv) a numbering of the flags at each vertex compatible with the cyclic order;
(v) a numbering of the edges by 1, . . . , e;


## Page 78


78
KAI CIELIEBAK AND EVGENY VOLKOV
(vi) an orientation of each edge.
We call the data (i) and (ii) a labelling, the additional data (iii) – (vi) an extension
of the labelling, and all data together an extended labelling. Note that a labelling is
uniquely determined by an ordered set of ℓleaves, the first ones on their boundary
components.
Remark 11.1. This notion of a labelling differs from the one in [10] where a “la-
belling” comprises items (i)–(iv). The reason for this is that the operations as-
sociated to ribbon graphs in [10] require a choice of items (i)–(iv), whereas the
operations in this paper require only a choice of items (i)–(ii).
An isomorphism Γ →Γ′ between labelled ribbon graphs is an isomorphism of ribbon
graphs which preserves the labellings. In other words, the induced homeomorphism
ΣΓ →ΣΓ′ matches the numberings of the boundary components and leaves.
Next, we introduce notation for certain isomorphism classes of connected ribbon
graphs and describe some standardizations on extensions of their labellings in the
presence of special vertices. We follow the notation of the previous subsection. In
particular, all the graphs are required to satisfy conditions (145) and (146).
Rooted trees.
Rooted planar trees will always be given the canonical labelling by
numbering leaves counterclockwise from the root. The set of isomorphism classes
of rooted planar trees with s leaves will be denoted by RTs. The corresponding set
of rooted trees with all nonroot vertices being trivalent will be called RT 3
s .
(s) Trivalent graphs.
The set of isomorphism classes of labelled connected
trivalent ribbon graphs without special vertices of genus g ≥0 with ℓ≥1 boundary
components will be denoted by Rℓ,g. This notation is borrowed from [15].
(o) Trivalent trees.
The set of isomorphism classes of labelled connected
trivalent ribbon trees without special vertices with s leaves will be denoted by Rs.
(i) Trees with one special vertex. Consider a labelled planar tree Γ with one
special vertex S of degree d. We number its rooted components in counterclockwise
order T1, . . . , Td where T1 contains the leaf number 1. We denote by A the special
flag given by the root of T1. We require that any extension of the labelling be
standardized as follows: the special vertex S is the last one in the numbering of
vertices, and the special flag A is the first one in the numbering of flags around S.
We call the labelling on Γ canonical if the induced labelling on T1 is the canonical
one as a rooted tree (i.e., leaf number 1 in the labelling comes right after the root
in the counterclockwise order on T1). We denote the set of isomorphism classes of
labelled planar trees with s leaves and one special d-valent vertex by Rs;d, and its
subset of such trees with canonical labelling by Rcan
s;d ⊂Rs;d.
(ii) Trees with two special vertices.
Given a labelled tree with two special
vertices, we require that any extension of the labelling be standardized as follows:
the special vertices S1, S2 are the last two in the numbering of the vertices, and the
special flags A and Z are the first ones in the numberings of flags around S1 and
S2. We denote the set of isomorphism classes of labelled trees with s leaves and
two special vertices of degrees d1, d2 by Rs;d1,d2.
(iv) Circular graphs with one special vertex. For an extended labelling on a
circular graph with one special vertex, we require that the special vertex S be the
last one in the numbering of vertices, and the special flag A be the first one in the


## Page 79

79
numbering of flags around S. We denote the set of isomorphism classes of labelled
circular graphs with one special d-valent vertex and s1, s2 leaves on the boundary
components by Rs1,s2;d.
Generalized labellings.
We will need one more variation of the concept of a
labelling. Consider a planar tree Γ with one special vertex. A generalized labelling
on Γ is a labelling together with a numbering of the special flags compatible with
their cyclic order. Thus, in contrast to the above standardization of an extended
labelling, this allows the special flag A to have any number (not necessarily 1).
An isomorphism of trees with generalized labellings is a tree isomorphism that
preserves the generalized labellings. The set of isomorpism classes of generalized
labelled trees with one special d-valent vertex and s leaves will be denoted by Rgen
s;d .
Observe that the cyclic group Zd acts freely on Rgen
s;d by cyclic renumbering of the
special flags, and the subset Rs;d ⊂Rgen
s;d (giving the special flag A number 1) is a
fundamental locus for this action.
Marked graphs.
An isomorphism class of marked ribbon graphs of a certain
type will be denoted by adding the upper index m to the corresponding notation
for the isomorphism class of graphs without marking, e.g. Rm
s1,s2;d. It is understood
that an isomorphism of marked graphs must map the marked edge to the marked
edge.
Edge and vertex order.
An extended labelling on a ribbon graph Γ gives rise
to two bijective maps (where f = |Flag(Γ)|)
Oe : {1, . . . , 2e, . . . , f} −→Flag(Γ),
Ov : {1, . . . , f} −→Flag(Γ).
The first one is called the edge order on Flag(Γ); it is determined by items (i), (ii),
(v) and (vi) of the extended labelling, mapping the numbers 1, . . . , 2e to the flags
corresponding to edges according to (v) and (vi), and the remaining numbers to
the leaves according to (i) and (ii). The second one is called the vertex order; it is
determined by items (iii) and (iv), numbering the flags in the order (iii) of vertices
and using the ordering (iv) at each vertex. By composition we obtain the reordering
permutation

Special labellings of o-marked ribbon graphs.
Consider a labelled ribbon
graph Γ with an o-marked edge l. The labelling is called special if it has the following
properties (see the right hand side of Figure 6):


## Page 81


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S ITERATED INTEGRALS
81
• The boundary component that runs parallel to l (in the direction of its
orientation) on its left has number 1 in the numbering of boundary com-
ponents. The first leaf on this boundary component encountered when
moving in the direction of the boundary orientation from the portion to
the left of l has number 1 in the numbering of its leaves.
• If the boundary component that runs parallel to l on its right differs from
the one on its left, then it has number 2 in the numbering of boundary
components. In this case, the first leaf on this boundary component en-
countered when moving in the direction of the boundary orientation from
the portion to the right of l has number 1 in the numbering of its leaves.
The sign exponent η3.
Definition 15. Let Γ be an extended labelled ribbon graph. We recall the basic
properties of η3(Γ) defined in [10, Appendix A] for connected Γ. The sign exponent
η3(Γ) does not depend on items (ii) and (iv) in Definition 14, and it changes by 1
under the following operations:
• swapping the order of two adjacent boundary components in (i);
• swapping the order of two adjacent vertices in (iii);
• swapping the order of two adjacent edges in (v);
• flipping the orientation of an edge in (vi).
For a disconnected graph Γ := Γ1⨿Γ2 with connected Γ1 and Γ2, we define η3(Γ) :=
η3(Γ1) + η3(Γ2) for the canonical disjoint union extended labelling of Γ and extend
the definition to all other extended labellings of Γ using the four items above.
11.4. Automorphisms. By definition, an automorphism of a (labelled) ribbon
graph is a self-isomorphism. The following simple result (Lemma 2.1 in [15]) is
crucial for the discussion of automorphisms.
Lemma 11.2. If an automorphism of a connected ribbon graph fixes at least one
flag, then it must be trivial.
Lemma 11.3. (a) Connected trees with two special vertices have no nontrivial
automorphisms.
(b) Any automorphism of a circular graph with one special vertex that preserves its
boundary components must be trivial.
Proof. For part (a), let ϕ be an automorphism of a tree Γ with two special vertices
S1, S2. Since the numbering of the special vertices is part of the structure of the
graph, we have ϕ(S1) = S1 and ϕ(S2) = S2. Since the subtree CΓ connecting S1
and S2 is uniquely defined, it must be preserved by ϕ. In particular the flags A
(adjacent to S1) and Z (adjacent to S2) of CΓ are fixed by ϕ. By Lemma 11.2, the
automorphism ϕ must be trivial.
For part (b), let ϕ be an automorphism of a circular graph Γ with one special vertex
S preserving the boundary components. Then the unique embedded cycle of Γ is
preserved by ϕ. Assume first that the special vertex S does not lie on the cycle.
Since A is the unique flag of S which is connected to the cycle via a chain of edges
not crossing S, we have ϕ(A) = A. Assume now that the special vertex S lies on
the cycle. Since ϕ preserves the boundary components, it preserves the orientation

11.5. Operations on ribbon graphs 1. In this subsection we describe three
operations on ribbon graphs: disjoint union, cutting, and gluing.
Disjoint union.
Let Γ1 and Γ2 be two connected (extended) labelled graphs.
Then the disjoint union Γ1 ⨿Γ2 inherits a natural (extended) labelling, putting Γ1
before Γ2 in all numberings. We will assume this (extended) labelling on Γ1 ⨿Γ2
unless otherwise specified.
Cutting and gluing (see Figure 6).
Given a marked graph (Γ, l) we define a
new graph Γ \ l by cutting open the marked edge l. Formally, this just means that
we remove the corresponding unordered pair l = {u, v} from the set of edges, so
the interior flags u, v become exterior ones. Note that the resulting graph Γ \ l is
not marked, but it has a distinguished unordered pair of leaves u, v.


## Page 83


STRING TOPOLOGY OPERATIONS UNDER CHEN’S ITERATED INTEGRALS
83
Conversely, given a graph Γ with a distinguished unordered pair of leaves u, v we
define a new graph Γ ∪{u, v} by gluing u and v to a new edge. Formally, this
just means that we add the unordered pair l := {u, v} to the set of edges, so the
exterior flags u, v become interior ones. Note that the resulting graph Γ ∪{u, v}
has no more distinguished unordered pair of leaves, by it is marked by the interior
edge l = {u, v}. The resulting cutting and gluing operations
{marked graphs} ←→{graphs with a given unordered pair of leaves}
are clearly inverse to each other, and they induce operations
{o-marked graphs} ←→{graphs with a given ordered pair of leaves}
Next we discuss how (extended) labellings get transferred under these operations.
Consider a graph Γ with a given ordered pair of leaves u, v. Suppose we are given a
labelling for Γ such that u comes before v in the ordering of the leaves, and denote
the difference of their positions by |v −u|. Assume that u, v lie either on the same
boundary component (in which case we require that u, v are not adjacent in the
cyclic order on that component), or on adjacent ones in the ordering of boundary
components. The glued graph Γ∪(u, v) inherits a labelling by requalifying the flags
u and v from “exterior” to “interior”, and keeping the ordering of the remaining
leaves.
Assume now that an extension of the labelling of Γ is given. Then the labelling of
the glued graph Γ ∪(u, v) inherits an extension by putting the new oriented edge
l = (u, v) in first position for the edge order. Note that the vertex order of flags
remains the same under the gluing operation. Since we must move u past |v−u|−1
leaves to put it next to v in the edge order, the sign exponents of the reordering
maps of the two graphs are related by
(154)
¯RΓ∪(u,v) ≡¯RΓ + |v −u| −1.
The preceding discussion gives rise to the following gluing operations.

11.6. Operations on ribbon graphs 2. In this subsection we introduce three
more operations: duality, attaching a leg, and attaching a tree.
Duality (see Figure 7).
The following duality operation on ribbon graphs plays
a crucial role in [15]. Let (Γ, l) be an o-marked labelled trivalent ribbon graph with
l = (u, v) the oriented marked nonspecial edge. Let (z, w, u) and (v, x, y) be the
two vertices connected by l as shown in Figure 7. We define the o-marked labelled
graph (I(Γ), I(l)) using the same set of flags, but assembling them into vertices
and edges slightly differently. Namely, we let I(l) := (u, v) be the oriented marked
edge of I(Γ) and (y, z, u) and (v, w, x) be its adjacent vertices, see Figure 7. The
other vertices and edges stay the same. Geometrically, the operation I is cutting
out a subtree with 4 leaves and pasting back the dual subtree. In particular, the
type of the graph remains the same. Since the two graphs have the same boundary
components and leaves, I(Γ) inherits the labelling from Γ.
Attaching a leg (see Figure 8).
Let Γ be a labelled graph with special vertices
and let
S = (f1, . . . , fd)
be a special vertex of Γ. Assume that d ≥2 and its flags are ordered according
to our standardization. Fix some j ∈{1, . . . , d}. We add two more flags aj and
bj to the set of flags of Γ and two more relations: an edge {aj, bj} and a vertex
[fj, fj+1, aj], where we set fj+1 := f1 for j = d. Note that the new ordered set of
special flags at S is
(f1, . . . , fj−1, bj, fj+2, . . . , fd),
so the flags fj and fj+1 have become nonspecial. If j = d, then the new ordered
set of special flags at S is
(bd, f2, . . . , fd−1).

Note that this ordering is compatible with our standardization of extensions of
labellings in §11.3. We call the resulting labelled graph Γj.
If the labelling of Γ is given an extension, then we give the labelling of Γj the
following extension: the new edge (aj, bj) is given the first position in the ordering
of edges and oriented as written, and the new vertex (fj, fj+1, aj) is given the last
number in the ordering of nonspecial vertices with its flags ordered as written.
Attaching a tree.
Let Γ be a graph and T a rooted tree (see the beginning
of §11.2 ). Let l be a leaf of Γ and v its vertex. The result of attaching T to Γ along
l is the quotient Γ ⨿T/ ∼, where the root vertex of T is identified with v and the
root flag of T is identified with l.
12. Integrals over configuration spaces
In this section we continue our discussion of integrals over configuration spaces.
Their analytical aspects were dealt with in §9 and §10. Now we give precise def-
initions with regards to signs and orientations, prove their independence of the
extension of a labelling, and discuss the effect of a change of labelling.
Throughout this section, M denotes a closed oriented manifold of dimension n, and
we use the notation and terminology from §11.
12.1. Configuration spaces associated to ribbon graphs. Let Γ be a ribbon

flags, by e the number of edges, by s the number of leaves, and by f the number
of flags. Recall from §11.3 the vertex and edge orders induced by the extended
labelling and the reordering permutation ¯RΓ.

For the proofs of our main results in §14 we will only need graphs Γ of the following

• (ii) trees with two special vertices (see Figure 4);
• (iii) circular graphs without special vertices;
• (iv) circular graphs with one special vertex (see Figure 5);
as well as disconnected graphs of the types (o) + (ii), (i) + (i), (o) + (iv), (i) + (iii).
For the definition of the Maurer-Cartan element in §12.5 we will need

g ≥0 with ℓ≥1 boundary components.
Recall from §11.2 that for the types of graphs above, all nonspecial vertices are
trivalent and each boundary component has at least one leaf ending on it. Note
that this implies conditions (169) and (172).
Remark 12.2. Recall that graphs of types (ii) and (iv) above have two distinguished

corresponding to A and Z are equal; in all other cases set D = ∅.
Then the
integrable forms on M d defined by (167) and (168) are actually smooth away from

Remark 12.11. Lemma 12.10 allows us to choose the extension of the labelling of
Γ as we please when integrating over the boundary of XΓ. This freedom will be
used e.g. for the cancellation of boundary strata in §14.3. If a graph appears as
the result of attaching a leg to some other graph, then the edge created as the
result of attaching comes first in the ordering of edges and is oriented towards the
corresponding special vertex. Moreover, the new vertex created as the result of
attaching comes last in the ordering of vertices, see §11.6. If a connected tree with
two special vertices has a doubly special edge, then the edge comes first in the
ordering of edges and is oriented from the first special vertex to the second one. If
a circular graph contains a doubly special edge, then it comes first in the ordering
of edges and is oriented by sliding it to the second boundary component of the
graph and picking up the boundary orientation, see §11.1.
13. The operations G, G2 and F2
In this section we define chain homotopies G2 and F2 that will be used in the
next section to prove compatibility for the product and the coproduct. Moreover,
we redefine the chain map G from equation (50) in a more convenient form and
establish some basic properties of the maps G, G2 and F2. We retain the notation
from the previous section. Thus M is a closed oriented n-dimensional manifold,
H ⊂Ω∗(M) a harmonic subspace, and eG an associated propagator. We abbreviate
Ω:= Ω∗(M).

14. Proof of the main theorem
In this section we prove Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2 from the Introduction.
In §14.1 we reduce Theorem 1.1 (restated as Theorem 14.1) to two chain level
statements, and in 14.2 we derive Corollary 1.2 (restated as Corollary 14.5). In §14.3
and §14.4 we prove the two chain level statements.

Remark 14.6. Recall that the differential pm
1,1,0 on Bcyc∗H is defined in terms of
rooted trees decorated with the wedge product at the vertices, the homotopy op-
erator P at the edges (including the root edge), the inclusion ι : H ,→Ωat the
leaves, and the projection π : Ω→H at the root vertex. This preserves the reduced
subspace Bcyc∗H of operations vanishing on words containing a 1 if P has the prop-
erties P ◦π = 0 and P ◦P = 0 (corresponding to a “special propagator” in the
terminology of [11]). Moreover, by [11, Corollary 6.20], the twisted operation pm
1,2,0
then descends to Bcyc∗H where it coincides with the untwisted operation p1,2,0.
Unfortunately, while P ◦π = 0 can always be achieved, we do not know how to
achieve P ◦P = 0 in our analytic setting. Therefore, we cannot assume that pm
1,1,0
preserves Bcyc∗H and apply the preceding argument. It is interesting to compare
this with Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 1.3 of [36], which provide the noncyclic version
of Corollary 14.5 above and have no twisting in the formula for the coproduct. The
explanation for this appears to be that the condition P ◦P = 0 can be arranged in
the algebraic approach of [36].
The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Propositions 14.2 and 14.3. The
proofs will be somewhat sketchy with respect to signs and orientations. Details on
signs and orientations can be found in [41].

Appendix A. Proof of Proposition 4.2
The proof of Proposition 4.2 follows by combining Jones’ article [24] and Good-
willie’s theorem [19]. It is most elegantly carried out in the formalism of mixed
complexes due to Kassel [26], which we will first recall following the notation in [14].

This formalism (due to H. Cartan) provides an alternative description of equivariant

in [14]). We denote the homology groups corresponding to this mixed complex by
